Steering notes

This segment has unusually strong cross-fire potential — all three experts want to quote the same three passages but for completely different reasons. Let that happen. When they all reach for 'plenteous flexible sympathy' or 'added soul', don't redirect; let them fight over what the passage actually means. The productive fault line is: Hartley says the double consciousness structure is Eliot's deliberate formal gambit; Blackstone says it's class analysis with spiritual window-dressing; Woodcourt says it's emotionally honest about moral dependency. None of them are wrong, which means they'll talk past each other unless pushed. Keep returning to: *does Eliot know what she's built?* That question destabilises all three readings. Resist the urge to resolve the Gwendolen question too early — it should simmer through the whole segment and only land in question 4.

Cross-engagement targets

  • Blackstone's claim that Deronda's certainty follows from material grounding (inheritance, institutional legitimacy) directly contradicts Hartley's claim that it's a formal/mythic effect of narrative withholding — both can't be right about what's *doing the work* in the transformation
  • Woodcourt's reading of the ending as genuinely tragic (Gwendolen loses her moral mirror) complements but complicates Hartley's 'lopsided architecture' claim — Hartley says Eliot *builds* the dependency deliberately; Woodcourt says Eliot then fails to resolve it honestly
  • Blackstone's question 'how many men get to be God for women?' about the 'brother in the stead of God' passage is a direct challenge to Woodcourt's reading of the same passage as Eliot's most honest insight about moral dependency — get them to confront this head-on
  • All three want to quote 'plenteous flexible sympathy' — Hartley reads it as Eliot diagnosing prose itself; Blackstone reads it as class diagnosis; Woodcourt reads it as felt experience rather than analysis. Letting them argue over *one sentence* will be more generative than moving to new material
Host (host)
We've been talking about the machinery of fate — the comic timing, the near-misses, the way Eliot engineers collisions between her characters. But underneath all that mechanism there's a stranger question: who, exactly, is this novel *about*? Eleanor, I want to start with you — and with a claim you made before we came on air. You said Deronda's transformation — the discovery of ancestry, the sudden purposefulness — is not psychological growth at all. You called it a magic trick. You said it's narrative architecture, not character development. Because on the surface it really does look like a man finding himself. So what's the trick?
Chapter 63, c63:p1
Chapter 63
Imagine the difference in Deronda’s state of mind when he left England and when he returned to it. He had set out for Genoa in total uncertainty how far the actual bent of his wishes and affections would be encouraged—how far the claims revealed to him might draw him into new paths, far away from the tracks his thoughts had lately been pursuing with a consent of desire which uncertainty made dangerous. He came back with something like a discovered charter warranting the inherited right that his ambition had begun to yearn for: he came back with what was better than freedom—with a duteous bond which his experience had been preparing him to accept gladly, even if it had been attended with no promise of satisfying a secret passionate longing never yet allowed to grow into a hope. But now he dared avow to himself the hidden selection of his love. Since the hour when he left the house at Chelsea in full-hearted silence under the effect of Mirah’s farewell look and words—their exquisite appealingness stirring in him that deep-laid care for womanhood which had begun when his own lip was like a girl’s—her hold on his feeling had helped him to be blameless in word and deed under the difficult circumstances we know of. There seemed no likelihood that he could ever woo this creature who had become dear to him amidst associations that forbade wooing; yet she had taken her place in his soul as a beloved type—reducing the power of other fascination and making a difference in it that became deficiency. The influence had been continually strengthened. It had lain in the course of poor Gwendolen’s lot that her dependence on Deronda tended to rouse in him the enthusiasm of self-martyring pity rather than of personal love, and his less constrained tenderness flowed with the fuller stream toward an indwelling image in all things unlike Gwendolen. Still more, his relation to Mordecai had brought with it a new nearness to Mirah which was not the less agitating because there was no apparent change in his position toward her; and she had inevitably been bound up in all the thoughts that made him shrink from an issue disappointing to her brother. This process had not gone on unconsciously in Deronda: he was conscious of it as we are of some covetousness that it would be better to nullify by encouraging other thoughts than to give it the insistency of confession even to ourselves: but the jealous fire had leaped out at Hans’s pretensions, and when his mother accused him of being in love with a Jewess any evasion suddenly seemed an infidelity. His mother had compelled him to a decisive acknowledgment of his love, as Joseph Kalonymos had compelled him to a definite expression of his resolve. This new state of decision wrought on Deronda with a force which surprised even himself. There was a release of all the energy which had long been spent in self-checking and suppression because of doubtful conditions; and he was ready to laugh at his own impetuosity when, as he neared England on his way from Mainz, he felt the remaining distance more and more of an obstruction. It was as if he had found an added soul in finding his ancestry—his judgment no longer wandering in the mazes of impartial sympathy, but choosing, with that partiality which is man’s best strength, the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical—exchanging that bird’s eye reasonableness which soars to avoid preference and loses all sense of quality for the generous reasonableness of drawing shoulder to shoulder with men of like inheritance. He wanted now to be again with Mordecai, to pour forth instead of restraining his feeling, to admit agreement and maintain dissent, and all the while to find Mirah’s presence without the embarrassment of obviously seeking it, to see her in the light of a new possibility, to interpret her looks and words from a new starting-point. He was not greatly alarmed about the effect of Hans’s attentions, but he had a presentiment that her feeling toward himself had from the first lain in a channel from which it was not likely to be diverted into love. To astonish a woman by turning into her lover when she has been thinking of you merely as a Lord Chancellor is what a man naturally shrinks from: he is anxious to create an easier transition.
Deronda returns to England transformed by his discovery of Jewish parentage and his love for Mirah, no longer constrained by uncertainty but energized by the fusion of inherited duty and personal desire.
Daniel DerondaMirah LapidothGwendolen HarlethMordecaiHans MeyrickJoseph Kalonymosidentityjudaismlovedutyinheritancenationalitytenderpolemical
Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
The trick is that Eliot tells you what to feel before you've had time to feel it yourself. She opens the Genoa return chapter with — and this is a deliberate rhetorical move — 'Imagine the difference in Deronda's state of mind.' That word 'imagine' is doing enormous structural work. She's outsourcing the emotional labour to us. And then she gives us the before: that extraordinary portrait in Chapter 32 of a man paralysed by his own sympathy. Listen to how she puts it — this is the diagnosis of Deronda before the transformation: 'His plenteous, flexible sympathy had ended by falling into one current with that reflective analysis which tends to neutralize sympathy.' That sentence is eating itself. Sympathy neutralizing sympathy. It's a formal enactment of the paralysis it's describing. And then in Chapter 63, she resolves it — surgically, almost too neatly — with the 'added soul' sentence. That resolution doesn't come from within Deronda. It comes from the plot delivering him an identity. That's the architecture.

Chapter 32, c32:p24
Chapter 32
It happened that the very vividness of his impressions had often made him the more enigmatic to his friends, and had contributed to an apparent indefiniteness in his sentiments. His early-wakened sensibility and reflectiveness had developed into a many-sided sympathy, which threatened to hinder any persistent course of action: as soon as he took up any antagonism, though only in thought, he seemed to himself like the Sabine warriors in the memorable story—with nothing to meet his spear but flesh of his flesh, and objects that he loved. His imagination had so wrought itself to the habit of seeing things as they probably appeared to others, that a strong partisanship, unless it were against an immediate oppression, had become an insincerity for him. His plenteous, flexible sympathy had ended by falling into one current with that reflective analysis which tends to neutralize sympathy. Few men were able to keep themselves clearer of vices than he; yet he hated vices mildly, being used to think of them less in the abstract than as a part of mixed human natures having an individual history, which it was the bent of his mind to trace with understanding and pity. With the same innate balance he was fervidly democratic in his feeling for the multitude, and yet, through his affections and imagination, intensely conservative; voracious of speculations on government and religion, yet loth to part with long-sanctioned forms which, for him, were quick with memories and sentiments that no argument could lay dead. We fall on the leaning side; and Deronda suspected himself of loving too well the losing causes of the world. Martyrdom changes sides, and he was in danger of changing with it, having a strong repugnance to taking up that clue of success which the order of the world often forces upon us and makes it treason against the common weal to reject. And yet his fear of falling into an unreasoning narrow hatred made a check for him: he apologized for the heirs of privilege; he shrank with dislike from the loser’s bitterness and the denunciatory tone of the unaccepted innovator. A too reflective and diffusive sympathy was in danger of paralyzing in him that indignation against wrong and that selectness of fellowship which are the conditions of moral force; and in the last few years of confirmed manhood he had become so keenly aware of this that what he most longed for was either some external event, or some inward light, that would urge him into a definite line of action, and compress his wandering energy. He was ceasing to care for knowledge—he had no ambition for practice—unless they could both be gathered up into one current with his emotions; and he dreaded, as if it were a dwelling-place of lost souls, that dead anatomy of culture which turns the universe into a mere ceaseless answer to queries, and knows, not everything, but everything else about everything—as if one should be ignorant of nothing concerning the scent of violets except the scent itself for which one had no nostril. But how and whence was the needed event to come?—the influence that would justify partiality, and make him what he longed to be, yet was unable to make himself—an organic part of social life, instead of roaming in it like a yearning disembodied spirit, stirred with a vague social passion, but without fixed local habitation to render fellowship real? To make a little difference for the better was what he was not contented to live without; but how to make it? It is one thing to see your road, another to cut it. He found some of the fault in his birth and the way he had been brought up, which had laid no special demands on him and had given him no fixed relationship except one of a doubtful kind; but he did not attempt to hide from himself that he had fallen into a meditative numbness, and was gliding farther and farther from that life of practically energetic sentiment which he would have proclaimed (if he had been inclined to proclaim anything) to be the best of all life, and for himself the only way worth living. He wanted some way of keeping emotion and its progeny of sentiments—which make the savors of life—substantial and strong in the face of a reflectiveness that threatened to nullify all differences. To pound the objects of sentiment into small dust, yet keep sentiment alive and active, was something like the famous recipe for making cannon—to first take a round hole and then enclose it with iron; whatever you do keeping fast hold of your round hole. Yet how distinguish what our will may wisely save in its completeness, from the heaping of cat-mummies and the expensive cult of enshrined putrefactions?
An intricate analysis of Deronda's psychological makeup: his sensitive responsiveness has produced paralysis of will, his conflicting political and emotional loyalties leave him without conviction, and his hunger is for a cause that would justify definitive action.
Daniel Derondaidentityvocationsympathyegoismdutypolemical
James Blackstone (social_historian)
I want to push on that, because I think the architecture argument is correct but perhaps too generous to Eliot. What Deronda actually receives in Genoa is not an identity in any spiritual sense. He receives property. He receives institutional legitimacy. There's a family chest. There are documents. There is Joseph Kalonymos, who compels him — the text uses that word, compels — to a definite expression of resolve. Now, the Chapter 32 portrait that Eleanor just quoted — that diagnosis of infinite sympathy collapsing into paralysis — that is, among other things, a portrait of what it looks like to be a man of means with no fixed calling. The passage even says he had 'no fixed relationship except one of a doubtful kind' and that his upbringing 'had laid no special demands on him.' 'He was ceasing to care for knowledge — he had no ambition for practice — unless they could both be gathered up into one current with his emotions.' This is the condition of a certain kind of educated, propertied Englishman in the 1860s and 1870s, adrift between institutions. What resolves his paralysis is not mystical ancestry. What resolves it is that he finally has a clear title — legally, socially — to a community and a cause. Whether Eliot sees that — whether she knows the material engine beneath the spiritual language — that is the interesting question.

He wanted now to be again with Mordecai, to pour forth instead of restraining his feeling, to admit agreement and maintain dissent, and all the while to find Mirah’s presence without the embarrassment of obviously seeking it, to see her in the light of a new possibility, to interpret her looks and words from a new starting-point. He was not greatly alarmed about the effect of Hans’s attentions, but he had a presentiment that her feeling toward himself had from the first lain in a channel from which it was not likely to be diverted into love. To astonish a woman by turning into her lover when she has been thinking of you merely as a Lord Chancellor is what a man naturally shrinks from: he is anxious to create an easier transition.
Deronda returns to England transformed by his discovery of Jewish parentage and his love for Mirah, no longer constrained by uncertainty but energized by the fusion of inherited duty and personal desire.

Chapter 32, c32:p24
Chapter 32
It happened that the very vividness of his impressions had often made him the more enigmatic to his friends, and had contributed to an apparent indefiniteness in his sentiments. His early-wakened sensibility and reflectiveness had developed into a many-sided sympathy, which threatened to hinder any persistent course of action: as soon as he took up any antagonism, though only in thought, he seemed to himself like the Sabine warriors in the memorable story—with nothing to meet his spear but flesh of his flesh, and objects that he loved. His imagination had so wrought itself to the habit of seeing things as they probably appeared to others, that a strong partisanship, unless it were against an immediate oppression, had become an insincerity for him. His plenteous, flexible sympathy had ended by falling into one current with that reflective analysis which tends to neutralize sympathy. Few men were able to keep themselves clearer of vices than he; yet he hated vices mildly, being used to think of them less in the abstract than as a part of mixed human natures having an individual history, which it was the bent of his mind to trace with understanding and pity. With the same innate balance he was fervidly democratic in his feeling for the multitude, and yet, through his affections and imagination, intensely conservative; voracious of speculations on government and religion, yet loth to part with long-sanctioned forms which, for him, were quick with memories and sentiments that no argument could lay dead. We fall on the leaning side; and Deronda suspected himself of loving too well the losing causes of the world. Martyrdom changes sides, and he was in danger of changing with it, having a strong repugnance to taking up that clue of success which the order of the world often forces upon us and makes it treason against the common weal to reject. And yet his fear of falling into an unreasoning narrow hatred made a check for him: he apologized for the heirs of privilege; he shrank with dislike from the loser’s bitterness and the denunciatory tone of the unaccepted innovator. A too reflective and diffusive sympathy was in danger of paralyzing in him that indignation against wrong and that selectness of fellowship which are the conditions of moral force; and in the last few years of confirmed manhood he had become so keenly aware of this that what he most longed for was either some external event, or some inward light, that would urge him into a definite line of action, and compress his wandering energy. He was ceasing to care for knowledge—he had no ambition for practice—unless they could both be gathered up into one current with his emotions; and he dreaded, as if it were a dwelling-place of lost souls, that dead anatomy of culture which turns the universe into a mere ceaseless answer to queries, and knows, not everything, but everything else about everything—as if one should be ignorant of nothing concerning the scent of violets except the scent itself for which one had no nostril. But how and whence was the needed event to come?—the influence that would justify partiality, and make him what he longed to be, yet was unable to make himself—an organic part of social life, instead of roaming in it like a yearning disembodied spirit, stirred with a vague social passion, but without fixed local habitation to render fellowship real? To make a little difference for the better was what he was not contented to live without; but how to make it? It is one thing to see your road, another to cut it. He found some of the fault in his birth and the way he had been brought up, which had laid no special demands on him and had given him no fixed relationship except one of a doubtful kind; but he did not attempt to hide from himself that he had fallen into a meditative numbness, and was gliding farther and farther from that life of practically energetic sentiment which he would have proclaimed (if he had been inclined to proclaim anything) to be the best of all life, and for himself the only way worth living. He wanted some way of keeping emotion and its progeny of sentiments—which make the savors of life—substantial and strong in the face of a reflectiveness that threatened to nullify all differences. To pound the objects of sentiment into small dust, yet keep sentiment alive and active, was something like the famous recipe for making cannon—to first take a round hole and then enclose it with iron; whatever you do keeping fast hold of your round hole. Yet how distinguish what our will may wisely save in its completeness, from the heaping of cat-mummies and the expensive cult of enshrined putrefactions?
An intricate analysis of Deronda's psychological makeup: his sensitive responsiveness has produced paralysis of will, his conflicting political and emotional loyalties leave him without conviction, and his hunger is for a cause that would justify definitive action.

Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
I don't think those two readings are incompatible. Eliot is not naive about the material. Look at the 'added soul' sentence itself — the full version, not just the headline: 'It was as if he had found an added soul in finding his ancestry — his judgment no longer wandering in the mazes of impartial sympathy, but choosing, with that partiality which is man's best strength, the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical.' 'Partiality which is man's best strength.' She is not endorsing universalism. She is arguing against it. The formal trick — if it is a trick — is that she spends thirty chapters building a man whose entire virtue is impartial sympathy, and then argues that partiality is better. That is either a devastating irony or a catastrophic contradiction, and I genuinely cannot decide which she intended.
Chapter 63, c63:p1
Chapter 63
Imagine the difference in Deronda’s state of mind when he left England and when he returned to it. He had set out for Genoa in total uncertainty how far the actual bent of his wishes and affections would be encouraged—how far the claims revealed to him might draw him into new paths, far away from the tracks his thoughts had lately been pursuing with a consent of desire which uncertainty made dangerous. He came back with something like a discovered charter warranting the inherited right that his ambition had begun to yearn for: he came back with what was better than freedom—with a duteous bond which his experience had been preparing him to accept gladly, even if it had been attended with no promise of satisfying a secret passionate longing never yet allowed to grow into a hope. But now he dared avow to himself the hidden selection of his love. Since the hour when he left the house at Chelsea in full-hearted silence under the effect of Mirah’s farewell look and words—their exquisite appealingness stirring in him that deep-laid care for womanhood which had begun when his own lip was like a girl’s—her hold on his feeling had helped him to be blameless in word and deed under the difficult circumstances we know of. There seemed no likelihood that he could ever woo this creature who had become dear to him amidst associations that forbade wooing; yet she had taken her place in his soul as a beloved type—reducing the power of other fascination and making a difference in it that became deficiency. The influence had been continually strengthened. It had lain in the course of poor Gwendolen’s lot that her dependence on Deronda tended to rouse in him the enthusiasm of self-martyring pity rather than of personal love, and his less constrained tenderness flowed with the fuller stream toward an indwelling image in all things unlike Gwendolen. Still more, his relation to Mordecai had brought with it a new nearness to Mirah which was not the less agitating because there was no apparent change in his position toward her; and she had inevitably been bound up in all the thoughts that made him shrink from an issue disappointing to her brother. This process had not gone on unconsciously in Deronda: he was conscious of it as we are of some covetousness that it would be better to nullify by encouraging other thoughts than to give it the insistency of confession even to ourselves: but the jealous fire had leaped out at Hans’s pretensions, and when his mother accused him of being in love with a Jewess any evasion suddenly seemed an infidelity. His mother had compelled him to a decisive acknowledgment of his love, as Joseph Kalonymos had compelled him to a definite expression of his resolve. This new state of decision wrought on Deronda with a force which surprised even himself. There was a release of all the energy which had long been spent in self-checking and suppression because of doubtful conditions; and he was ready to laugh at his own impetuosity when, as he neared England on his way from Mainz, he felt the remaining distance more and more of an obstruction. It was as if he had found an added soul in finding his ancestry—his judgment no longer wandering in the mazes of impartial sympathy, but choosing, with that partiality which is man’s best strength, the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical—exchanging that bird’s eye reasonableness which soars to avoid preference and loses all sense of quality for the generous reasonableness of drawing shoulder to shoulder with men of like inheritance. He wanted now to be again with Mordecai, to pour forth instead of restraining his feeling, to admit agreement and maintain dissent, and all the while to find Mirah’s presence without the embarrassment of obviously seeking it, to see her in the light of a new possibility, to interpret her looks and words from a new starting-point. He was not greatly alarmed about the effect of Hans’s attentions, but he had a presentiment that her feeling toward himself had from the first lain in a channel from which it was not likely to be diverted into love. To astonish a woman by turning into her lover when she has been thinking of you merely as a Lord Chancellor is what a man naturally shrinks from: he is anxious to create an easier transition.
Deronda returns to England transformed by his discovery of Jewish parentage and his love for Mirah, no longer constrained by uncertainty but energized by the fusion of inherited duty and personal desire.

James Blackstone (social_historian)
I think she sees it — and she does it anyway. And that is precisely where the novel is at its most honest and most evasive simultaneously. Chapter 55 is the key passage for me. After the meeting with his mother, Deronda is described as a man who has 'joined the wrong band of wanderers.' 'He beheld the world changed for him by the certitude of ties that altered the poise of hopes and fears, and gave him a new sense of fellowship, as if under cover of the night he had joined the wrong band of wanderers, and found with the rise of morning that the tents of his kindred were grouped far off.' That is genuinely beautiful language. I'm not disputing the beauty. But notice what is actually happening in the plot at this moment: he has just received confirmation of parentage, of a family archive, of institutional membership in a community with historical claims. The image of tents grouped far off — that is Biblical, that is deeply felt — but the tents contain a chest. A documented inheritance. The poetry is doing ideological cover for the property.

Chapter 55, c55:p2
Chapter 55
In two hours after his parting from her he knew that the Princess Halm-Eberstein had left the hotel, and so far as the purpose of his journey to Genoa was concerned, he might himself have set off on his way to Mainz, to deliver the letter from Joseph Kalonymos, and get possession of the family chest. But mixed mental conditions, which did not resolve themselves into definite reasons, hindered him from departure. Long after the farewell he was kept passive by a weight of retrospective feeling. He lived again, with the new keenness of emotive memory, through the exciting scenes which seemed past only in the sense of preparation for their actual presence in his soul. He allowed himself in his solitude to sob, with perhaps more than a woman’s acuteness of compassion, over that woman’s life so near to his, and yet so remote. He beheld the world changed for him by the certitude of ties that altered the poise of hopes and fears, and gave him a new sense of fellowship, as if under cover of the night he had joined the wrong band of wanderers, and found with the rise of morning that the tents of his kindred were grouped far off. He had a quivering imaginative sense of close relation to the grandfather who had been animated by strong impulses and beloved thoughts, which were now perhaps being roused from their slumber within himself. And through all this passionate meditation Mordecai and Mirah were always present, as beings who clasped hands with him in sympathetic silence.
Deronda is held in place by overwhelming retrospective emotion after meeting his mother; he experiences profound psychological transformation through recognition of his Jewish heritage and reconnection with his grandfather's legacy, supported spiritually by Mordecai and Mirah.
Daniel DerondaPrincess Halm-EbersteinJoseph KalonymosMordecaiMirah Lapidothidentityjudaisminheritancesympathykinshiptendergothictragic
Caroline Woodcourt (close_reader)
I hear what James is saying, and I don't disagree exactly — but I think we're in danger of being more detached from the novel than it allows. That passage in Chapter 55 — 'he allowed himself in his solitude to sob, with perhaps more than a woman's acuteness of compassion, over that woman's life so near to his, and yet so remote' — that is not property. That is grief. And the reason I keep coming back to Gwendolen is precisely because the novel has a second consciousness running alongside Deronda's — and that consciousness is not resolved. It's abandoned. Both Eleanor and James are treating this as fundamentally Deronda's novel. But I'd argue Gwendolen is where the real psychological work is being done.

Chapter 64, c64:p46
Chapter 64
Meanwhile Gwendolen sat by like one who had visited the spirit-world and was full to the lips of an unutterable experience that threw a strange unreality over all the talk she was hearing of her own and the world’s business; and Mrs. Davilow was chiefly occupied in imagining what her daughter was feeling, and in wondering what was signified by her hinted doubt whether she would accept her husband’s bequest. Gwendolen in fact had before her the unsealed wall of an immediate purpose shutting off every other resolution. How to scale the wall? She wanted again to see and consult Deronda, that she might secure herself against any act he would disapprove. Would her remorse have maintained its power within her, or would she have felt absolved by secrecy, if it had not been for that outer conscience which was made for her by Deronda? It is hard to say how much we could forgive ourselves if we were secure from judgment by another whose opinion is the breathing-medium of all our joy—who brings to us with close pressure and immediate sequence that judgment of the Invisible and Universal which self-flattery and the world’s tolerance would easily melt and disperse. In this way our brother may be in the stead of God to us, and his opinion which has pierced even to the joints and marrow, may be our virtue in the making. That mission of Deronda to Gwendolen had begun with what she had felt to be his judgment of her at the gaming-table. He might easily have spoiled it:—much of our lives is spent in marring our own influence and turning others’ belief in us into a widely concluding unbelief which they call knowledge of the world, while it is really disappointment in you or me. Deronda had not spoiled his mission.
The narrator reflects on Gwendolen's dependence on Deronda's judgment as her moral compass, suggesting that another person can function as the conscience that prevents self-deception and permits genuine moral growth.
Gwendolen HarlethDaniel DerondaMrs. Davilowconsciencejudgmentmoralitydependencevirtuetragicpolemical

Because depending on how you read it, that's either the novel's most profound insight or its most alarming one.

Caroline Woodcourt (close_reader)
Let me read the full passage, because the sentence before it is just as important. 'It is hard to say how much we could forgive ourselves if we were secure from judgment by another whose opinion is the breathing-medium of all our joy — who brings to us with close pressure and immediate sequence that judgment of the Invisible and Universal which self-flattery and the world's tolerance would easily melt and disperse.' 'In this way our brother may be in the stead of God to us, and his opinion which has pierced even to the joints and marrow, may be our virtue in the making.' What Eliot is doing there is, I think, genuinely honest about how moral life actually works for most people — not through abstract principle but through the face of someone whose opinion you cannot bear to lose. The 'breathing-medium of all our joy' — that phrase stops me every time. Joy requiring another person as its atmosphere. But is she endorsing it? I think she is describing it with devastating clarity and leaving the question of whether it is a trap or a gift absolutely open.
Chapter 64, c64:p46
Chapter 64
Meanwhile Gwendolen sat by like one who had visited the spirit-world and was full to the lips of an unutterable experience that threw a strange unreality over all the talk she was hearing of her own and the world’s business; and Mrs. Davilow was chiefly occupied in imagining what her daughter was feeling, and in wondering what was signified by her hinted doubt whether she would accept her husband’s bequest. Gwendolen in fact had before her the unsealed wall of an immediate purpose shutting off every other resolution. How to scale the wall? She wanted again to see and consult Deronda, that she might secure herself against any act he would disapprove. Would her remorse have maintained its power within her, or would she have felt absolved by secrecy, if it had not been for that outer conscience which was made for her by Deronda? It is hard to say how much we could forgive ourselves if we were secure from judgment by another whose opinion is the breathing-medium of all our joy—who brings to us with close pressure and immediate sequence that judgment of the Invisible and Universal which self-flattery and the world’s tolerance would easily melt and disperse. In this way our brother may be in the stead of God to us, and his opinion which has pierced even to the joints and marrow, may be our virtue in the making. That mission of Deronda to Gwendolen had begun with what she had felt to be his judgment of her at the gaming-table. He might easily have spoiled it:—much of our lives is spent in marring our own influence and turning others’ belief in us into a widely concluding unbelief which they call knowledge of the world, while it is really disappointment in you or me. Deronda had not spoiled his mission.
The narrator reflects on Gwendolen's dependence on Deronda's judgment as her moral compass, suggesting that another person can function as the conscience that prevents self-deception and permits genuine moral growth.
Gwendolen HarlethDaniel DerondaMrs. Davilowconsciencejudgmentmoralitydependencevirtuetragicpolemical
James Blackstone (social_historian)
But that openness isn't neutral. Because who gets to be God for whom is not a random assignment. How many men in this novel serve as the 'breathing-medium' of a woman's moral life? And how many women serve in that capacity for a man? Deronda gets Mordecai as his spiritual interlocutor and Mirah as his emotional lodestone. Gwendolen gets Deronda — and then Deronda leaves. The passage calls Deronda Gwendolen's 'outer conscience.' That is a function. And when the function emigrates to Palestine, Gwendolen is left without her conscience's external scaffolding. Now — is Eliot aware of this asymmetry? I think she is. I think the novel enacts it. But I'm not convinced she regards it as a problem.

Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
James, I think you've put your finger on something precise — the asymmetry is structural, not accidental. Eliot has built two plots that require each other for their meaning and then separated them at the end. Deronda's plot is resolved — inheritance, vocation, Mirah, Mordecai, the mission to Judea. Everything clicks into alignment. Gwendolen's plot is left at the wall. The text even uses that image — 'the unsealed wall of an immediate purpose shutting off every other resolution.' 'How to scale the wall?' That's Gwendolen's question at the end. And the novel gives her no ladder. Is that Eliot's radical honesty — that some consciousnesses don't get resolution — or is it lopsided architecture she didn't fully reckon with? I find myself unable to prosecute a clean verdict.

Chapter 63, c63:p1
Chapter 63
Imagine the difference in Deronda’s state of mind when he left England and when he returned to it. He had set out for Genoa in total uncertainty how far the actual bent of his wishes and affections would be encouraged—how far the claims revealed to him might draw him into new paths, far away from the tracks his thoughts had lately been pursuing with a consent of desire which uncertainty made dangerous. He came back with something like a discovered charter warranting the inherited right that his ambition had begun to yearn for: he came back with what was better than freedom—with a duteous bond which his experience had been preparing him to accept gladly, even if it had been attended with no promise of satisfying a secret passionate longing never yet allowed to grow into a hope. But now he dared avow to himself the hidden selection of his love. Since the hour when he left the house at Chelsea in full-hearted silence under the effect of Mirah’s farewell look and words—their exquisite appealingness stirring in him that deep-laid care for womanhood which had begun when his own lip was like a girl’s—her hold on his feeling had helped him to be blameless in word and deed under the difficult circumstances we know of. There seemed no likelihood that he could ever woo this creature who had become dear to him amidst associations that forbade wooing; yet she had taken her place in his soul as a beloved type—reducing the power of other fascination and making a difference in it that became deficiency. The influence had been continually strengthened. It had lain in the course of poor Gwendolen’s lot that her dependence on Deronda tended to rouse in him the enthusiasm of self-martyring pity rather than of personal love, and his less constrained tenderness flowed with the fuller stream toward an indwelling image in all things unlike Gwendolen. Still more, his relation to Mordecai had brought with it a new nearness to Mirah which was not the less agitating because there was no apparent change in his position toward her; and she had inevitably been bound up in all the thoughts that made him shrink from an issue disappointing to her brother. This process had not gone on unconsciously in Deronda: he was conscious of it as we are of some covetousness that it would be better to nullify by encouraging other thoughts than to give it the insistency of confession even to ourselves: but the jealous fire had leaped out at Hans’s pretensions, and when his mother accused him of being in love with a Jewess any evasion suddenly seemed an infidelity. His mother had compelled him to a decisive acknowledgment of his love, as Joseph Kalonymos had compelled him to a definite expression of his resolve. This new state of decision wrought on Deronda with a force which surprised even himself. There was a release of all the energy which had long been spent in self-checking and suppression because of doubtful conditions; and he was ready to laugh at his own impetuosity when, as he neared England on his way from Mainz, he felt the remaining distance more and more of an obstruction. It was as if he had found an added soul in finding his ancestry—his judgment no longer wandering in the mazes of impartial sympathy, but choosing, with that partiality which is man’s best strength, the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical—exchanging that bird’s eye reasonableness which soars to avoid preference and loses all sense of quality for the generous reasonableness of drawing shoulder to shoulder with men of like inheritance. He wanted now to be again with Mordecai, to pour forth instead of restraining his feeling, to admit agreement and maintain dissent, and all the while to find Mirah’s presence without the embarrassment of obviously seeking it, to see her in the light of a new possibility, to interpret her looks and words from a new starting-point. He was not greatly alarmed about the effect of Hans’s attentions, but he had a presentiment that her feeling toward himself had from the first lain in a channel from which it was not likely to be diverted into love. To astonish a woman by turning into her lover when she has been thinking of you merely as a Lord Chancellor is what a man naturally shrinks from: he is anxious to create an easier transition.
Deronda returns to England transformed by his discovery of Jewish parentage and his love for Mirah, no longer constrained by uncertainty but energized by the fusion of inherited duty and personal desire.

Caroline Woodcourt (close_reader)
I'll defend it — partially. What Eliot is doing in the Gwendolen passages is not presenting resolution. She's presenting the problem of consciousness that has been oriented around another person and must now reorient itself. That is not a comfortable ending. It is an honest one. And I think it's braver than giving Gwendolen a tidy conclusion. The tragedy is real. But it's Eliot trusting us to understand that 'virtue in the making' is unfinished by definition.

James Blackstone (social_historian)
I'm not prepared to defend it — but I want to be precise about what I'm objecting to. It is not that Gwendolen is left without resolution. Caroline Woodcourt (close_reader)
I think the novel argues that sympathy — truly expansive, truly reflective sympathy — is unsustainable as a way of living. The Chapter 32 passage is almost a clinical document of what happens when sympathy becomes its own negation — when you understand everyone so well that you can commit to no one. Eliot's argument — as I read it — is that you have to *choose*, knowing the choice diminishes you. Deronda chooses. And what he loses is the very quality that made Gwendolen able to grow. So yes. The novel's final argument might be: partiality is necessary, and it costs something irreplaceable.
Chapter 32, c32:p24
Chapter 32
It happened that the very vividness of his impressions had often made him the more enigmatic to his friends, and had contributed to an apparent indefiniteness in his sentiments. His early-wakened sensibility and reflectiveness had developed into a many-sided sympathy, which threatened to hinder any persistent course of action: as soon as he took up any antagonism, though only in thought, he seemed to himself like the Sabine warriors in the memorable story—with nothing to meet his spear but flesh of his flesh, and objects that he loved. His imagination had so wrought itself to the habit of seeing things as they probably appeared to others, that a strong partisanship, unless it were against an immediate oppression, had become an insincerity for him. His plenteous, flexible sympathy had ended by falling into one current with that reflective analysis which tends to neutralize sympathy. Few men were able to keep themselves clearer of vices than he; yet he hated vices mildly, being used to think of them less in the abstract than as a part of mixed human natures having an individual history, which it was the bent of his mind to trace with understanding and pity. With the same innate balance he was fervidly democratic in his feeling for the multitude, and yet, through his affections and imagination, intensely conservative; voracious of speculations on government and religion, yet loth to part with long-sanctioned forms which, for him, were quick with memories and sentiments that no argument could lay dead. We fall on the leaning side; and Deronda suspected himself of loving too well the losing causes of the world. Martyrdom changes sides, and he was in danger of changing with it, having a strong repugnance to taking up that clue of success which the order of the world often forces upon us and makes it treason against the common weal to reject. And yet his fear of falling into an unreasoning narrow hatred made a check for him: he apologized for the heirs of privilege; he shrank with dislike from the loser’s bitterness and the denunciatory tone of the unaccepted innovator. A too reflective and diffusive sympathy was in danger of paralyzing in him that indignation against wrong and that selectness of fellowship which are the conditions of moral force; and in the last few years of confirmed manhood he had become so keenly aware of this that what he most longed for was either some external event, or some inward light, that would urge him into a definite line of action, and compress his wandering energy. He was ceasing to care for knowledge—he had no ambition for practice—unless they could both be gathered up into one current with his emotions; and he dreaded, as if it were a dwelling-place of lost souls, that dead anatomy of culture which turns the universe into a mere ceaseless answer to queries, and knows, not everything, but everything else about everything—as if one should be ignorant of nothing concerning the scent of violets except the scent itself for which one had no nostril. But how and whence was the needed event to come?—the influence that would justify partiality, and make him what he longed to be, yet was unable to make himself—an organic part of social life, instead of roaming in it like a yearning disembodied spirit, stirred with a vague social passion, but without fixed local habitation to render fellowship real? To make a little difference for the better was what he was not contented to live without; but how to make it? It is one thing to see your road, another to cut it. He found some of the fault in his birth and the way he had been brought up, which had laid no special demands on him and had given him no fixed relationship except one of a doubtful kind; but he did not attempt to hide from himself that he had fallen into a meditative numbness, and was gliding farther and farther from that life of practically energetic sentiment which he would have proclaimed (if he had been inclined to proclaim anything) to be the best of all life, and for himself the only way worth living. He wanted some way of keeping emotion and its progeny of sentiments—which make the savors of life—substantial and strong in the face of a reflectiveness that threatened to nullify all differences. To pound the objects of sentiment into small dust, yet keep sentiment alive and active, was something like the famous recipe for making cannon—to first take a round hole and then enclose it with iron; whatever you do keeping fast hold of your round hole. Yet how distinguish what our will may wisely save in its completeness, from the heaping of cat-mummies and the expensive cult of enshrined putrefactions?
An intricate analysis of Deronda's psychological makeup: his sensitive responsiveness has produced paralysis of will, his conflicting political and emotional loyalties leave him without conviction, and his hunger is for a cause that would justify definitive action.
Daniel Derondaidentityvocationsympathyegoismdutypolemical
Chapter 63, c63:p1
Chapter 63
Imagine the difference in Deronda’s state of mind when he left England and when he returned to it. He had set out for Genoa in total uncertainty how far the actual bent of his wishes and affections would be encouraged—how far the claims revealed to him might draw him into new paths, far away from the tracks his thoughts had lately been pursuing with a consent of desire which uncertainty made dangerous. He came back with something like a discovered charter warranting the inherited right that his ambition had begun to yearn for: he came back with what was better than freedom—with a duteous bond which his experience had been preparing him to accept gladly, even if it had been attended with no promise of satisfying a secret passionate longing never yet allowed to grow into a hope. But now he dared avow to himself the hidden selection of his love. Since the hour when he left the house at Chelsea in full-hearted silence under the effect of Mirah’s farewell look and words—their exquisite appealingness stirring in him that deep-laid care for womanhood which had begun when his own lip was like a girl’s—her hold on his feeling had helped him to be blameless in word and deed under the difficult circumstances we know of. There seemed no likelihood that he could ever woo this creature who had become dear to him amidst associations that forbade wooing; yet she had taken her place in his soul as a beloved type—reducing the power of other fascination and making a difference in it that became deficiency. The influence had been continually strengthened. It had lain in the course of poor Gwendolen’s lot that her dependence on Deronda tended to rouse in him the enthusiasm of self-martyring pity rather than of personal love, and his less constrained tenderness flowed with the fuller stream toward an indwelling image in all things unlike Gwendolen. Still more, his relation to Mordecai had brought with it a new nearness to Mirah which was not the less agitating because there was no apparent change in his position toward her; and she had inevitably been bound up in all the thoughts that made him shrink from an issue disappointing to her brother. This process had not gone on unconsciously in Deronda: he was conscious of it as we are of some covetousness that it would be better to nullify by encouraging other thoughts than to give it the insistency of confession even to ourselves: but the jealous fire had leaped out at Hans’s pretensions, and when his mother accused him of being in love with a Jewess any evasion suddenly seemed an infidelity. His mother had compelled him to a decisive acknowledgment of his love, as Joseph Kalonymos had compelled him to a definite expression of his resolve. This new state of decision wrought on Deronda with a force which surprised even himself. There was a release of all the energy which had long been spent in self-checking and suppression because of doubtful conditions; and he was ready to laugh at his own impetuosity when, as he neared England on his way from Mainz, he felt the remaining distance more and more of an obstruction. It was as if he had found an added soul in finding his ancestry—his judgment no longer wandering in the mazes of impartial sympathy, but choosing, with that partiality which is man’s best strength, the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical—exchanging that bird’s eye reasonableness which soars to avoid preference and loses all sense of quality for the generous reasonableness of drawing shoulder to shoulder with men of like inheritance. He wanted now to be again with Mordecai, to pour forth instead of restraining his feeling, to admit agreement and maintain dissent, and all the while to find Mirah’s presence without the embarrassment of obviously seeking it, to see her in the light of a new possibility, to interpret her looks and words from a new starting-point. He was not greatly alarmed about the effect of Hans’s attentions, but he had a presentiment that her feeling toward himself had from the first lain in a channel from which it was not likely to be diverted into love. To astonish a woman by turning into her lover when she has been thinking of you merely as a Lord Chancellor is what a man naturally shrinks from: he is anxious to create an easier transition.
Deronda returns to England transformed by his discovery of Jewish parentage and his love for Mirah, no longer constrained by uncertainty but energized by the fusion of inherited duty and personal desire.
Daniel DerondaMirah LapidothGwendolen HarlethMordecaiHans MeyrickJoseph Kalonymosidentityjudaismlovedutyinheritancenationalitytenderpolemical
Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
I'd put it differently. The novel doesn't argue — it demonstrates, structurally. The Chapter 32 portrait of paralysed sympathy is the condition of every serious modern consciousness — not just Deronda's. That's why it reads so immediately, so personally. The 'added soul' is Eliot's formal solution to a problem she's diagnosed with devastating accuracy: how do you make a morally serious person capable of action? Her answer is: you give him an inheritance that converts paralysis into purpose. Whether that's wish-fulfilment or prophecy — I think the novel holds both possibilities, deliberately, without resolving them.
Chapter 32, c32:p24
Chapter 32
It happened that the very vividness of his impressions had often made him the more enigmatic to his friends, and had contributed to an apparent indefiniteness in his sentiments. His early-wakened sensibility and reflectiveness had developed into a many-sided sympathy, which threatened to hinder any persistent course of action: as soon as he took up any antagonism, though only in thought, he seemed to himself like the Sabine warriors in the memorable story—with nothing to meet his spear but flesh of his flesh, and objects that he loved. His imagination had so wrought itself to the habit of seeing things as they probably appeared to others, that a strong partisanship, unless it were against an immediate oppression, had become an insincerity for him. His plenteous, flexible sympathy had ended by falling into one current with that reflective analysis which tends to neutralize sympathy. Few men were able to keep themselves clearer of vices than he; yet he hated vices mildly, being used to think of them less in the abstract than as a part of mixed human natures having an individual history, which it was the bent of his mind to trace with understanding and pity. With the same innate balance he was fervidly democratic in his feeling for the multitude, and yet, through his affections and imagination, intensely conservative; voracious of speculations on government and religion, yet loth to part with long-sanctioned forms which, for him, were quick with memories and sentiments that no argument could lay dead. We fall on the leaning side; and Deronda suspected himself of loving too well the losing causes of the world. Martyrdom changes sides, and he was in danger of changing with it, having a strong repugnance to taking up that clue of success which the order of the world often forces upon us and makes it treason against the common weal to reject. And yet his fear of falling into an unreasoning narrow hatred made a check for him: he apologized for the heirs of privilege; he shrank with dislike from the loser’s bitterness and the denunciatory tone of the unaccepted innovator. A too reflective and diffusive sympathy was in danger of paralyzing in him that indignation against wrong and that selectness of fellowship which are the conditions of moral force; and in the last few years of confirmed manhood he had become so keenly aware of this that what he most longed for was either some external event, or some inward light, that would urge him into a definite line of action, and compress his wandering energy. He was ceasing to care for knowledge—he had no ambition for practice—unless they could both be gathered up into one current with his emotions; and he dreaded, as if it were a dwelling-place of lost souls, that dead anatomy of culture which turns the universe into a mere ceaseless answer to queries, and knows, not everything, but everything else about everything—as if one should be ignorant of nothing concerning the scent of violets except the scent itself for which one had no nostril. But how and whence was the needed event to come?—the influence that would justify partiality, and make him what he longed to be, yet was unable to make himself—an organic part of social life, instead of roaming in it like a yearning disembodied spirit, stirred with a vague social passion, but without fixed local habitation to render fellowship real? To make a little difference for the better was what he was not contented to live without; but how to make it? It is one thing to see your road, another to cut it. He found some of the fault in his birth and the way he had been brought up, which had laid no special demands on him and had given him no fixed relationship except one of a doubtful kind; but he did not attempt to hide from himself that he had fallen into a meditative numbness, and was gliding farther and farther from that life of practically energetic sentiment which he would have proclaimed (if he had been inclined to proclaim anything) to be the best of all life, and for himself the only way worth living. He wanted some way of keeping emotion and its progeny of sentiments—which make the savors of life—substantial and strong in the face of a reflectiveness that threatened to nullify all differences. To pound the objects of sentiment into small dust, yet keep sentiment alive and active, was something like the famous recipe for making cannon—to first take a round hole and then enclose it with iron; whatever you do keeping fast hold of your round hole. Yet how distinguish what our will may wisely save in its completeness, from the heaping of cat-mummies and the expensive cult of enshrined putrefactions?
An intricate analysis of Deronda's psychological makeup: his sensitive responsiveness has produced paralysis of will, his conflicting political and emotional loyalties leave him without conviction, and his hunger is for a cause that would justify definitive action.
Daniel Derondaidentityvocationsympathyegoismdutypolemical
Chapter 63, c63:p1
Chapter 63
Imagine the difference in Deronda’s state of mind when he left England and when he returned to it. He had set out for Genoa in total uncertainty how far the actual bent of his wishes and affections would be encouraged—how far the claims revealed to him might draw him into new paths, far away from the tracks his thoughts had lately been pursuing with a consent of desire which uncertainty made dangerous. He came back with something like a discovered charter warranting the inherited right that his ambition had begun to yearn for: he came back with what was better than freedom—with a duteous bond which his experience had been preparing him to accept gladly, even if it had been attended with no promise of satisfying a secret passionate longing never yet allowed to grow into a hope. But now he dared avow to himself the hidden selection of his love. Since the hour when he left the house at Chelsea in full-hearted silence under the effect of Mirah’s farewell look and words—their exquisite appealingness stirring in him that deep-laid care for womanhood which had begun when his own lip was like a girl’s—her hold on his feeling had helped him to be blameless in word and deed under the difficult circumstances we know of. There seemed no likelihood that he could ever woo this creature who had become dear to him amidst associations that forbade wooing; yet she had taken her place in his soul as a beloved type—reducing the power of other fascination and making a difference in it that became deficiency. The influence had been continually strengthened. It had lain in the course of poor Gwendolen’s lot that her dependence on Deronda tended to rouse in him the enthusiasm of self-martyring pity rather than of personal love, and his less constrained tenderness flowed with the fuller stream toward an indwelling image in all things unlike Gwendolen. Still more, his relation to Mordecai had brought with it a new nearness to Mirah which was not the less agitating because there was no apparent change in his position toward her; and she had inevitably been bound up in all the thoughts that made him shrink from an issue disappointing to her brother. This process had not gone on unconsciously in Deronda: he was conscious of it as we are of some covetousness that it would be better to nullify by encouraging other thoughts than to give it the insistency of confession even to ourselves: but the jealous fire had leaped out at Hans’s pretensions, and when his mother accused him of being in love with a Jewess any evasion suddenly seemed an infidelity. His mother had compelled him to a decisive acknowledgment of his love, as Joseph Kalonymos had compelled him to a definite expression of his resolve. This new state of decision wrought on Deronda with a force which surprised even himself. There was a release of all the energy which had long been spent in self-checking and suppression because of doubtful conditions; and he was ready to laugh at his own impetuosity when, as he neared England on his way from Mainz, he felt the remaining distance more and more of an obstruction. It was as if he had found an added soul in finding his ancestry—his judgment no longer wandering in the mazes of impartial sympathy, but choosing, with that partiality which is man’s best strength, the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical—exchanging that bird’s eye reasonableness which soars to avoid preference and loses all sense of quality for the generous reasonableness of drawing shoulder to shoulder with men of like inheritance. He wanted now to be again with Mordecai, to pour forth instead of restraining his feeling, to admit agreement and maintain dissent, and all the while to find Mirah’s presence without the embarrassment of obviously seeking it, to see her in the light of a new possibility, to interpret her looks and words from a new starting-point. He was not greatly alarmed about the effect of Hans’s attentions, but he had a presentiment that her feeling toward himself had from the first lain in a channel from which it was not likely to be diverted into love. To astonish a woman by turning into her lover when she has been thinking of you merely as a Lord Chancellor is what a man naturally shrinks from: he is anxious to create an easier transition.
Deronda returns to England transformed by his discovery of Jewish parentage and his love for Mirah, no longer constrained by uncertainty but energized by the fusion of inherited duty and personal desire.

James Blackstone (social_historian)
My position is simpler, and perhaps more uncomfortable. Eliot knows exactly what she's built. She diagnoses, with forensic precision, a society in which the material conditions of identity are distributed unequally — and then she gives her protagonist the one thing that resolves those conditions. She cannot give the same thing to Gwendolen — because the novel's world doesn't have it to give. And so what Eliot builds is not a flaw. It's a document. The novel tells us what it cost — what it still costs — to distribute conscience and purpose as unequally as it distributes property.

Chapter 64, c64:p46
Chapter 64
Meanwhile Gwendolen sat by like one who had visited the spirit-world and was full to the lips of an unutterable experience that threw a strange unreality over all the talk she was hearing of her own and the world’s business; and Mrs. Davilow was chiefly occupied in imagining what her daughter was feeling, and in wondering what was signified by her hinted doubt whether she would accept her husband’s bequest. Gwendolen in fact had before her the unsealed wall of an immediate purpose shutting off every other resolution. How to scale the wall? She wanted again to see and consult Deronda, that she might secure herself against any act he would disapprove. Would her remorse have maintained its power within her, or would she have felt absolved by secrecy, if it had not been for that outer conscience which was made for her by Deronda? It is hard to say how much we could forgive ourselves if we were secure from judgment by another whose opinion is the breathing-medium of all our joy—who brings to us with close pressure and immediate sequence that judgment of the Invisible and Universal which self-flattery and the world’s tolerance would easily melt and disperse. In this way our brother may be in the stead of God to us, and his opinion which has pierced even to the joints and marrow, may be our virtue in the making. That mission of Deronda to Gwendolen had begun with what she had felt to be his judgment of her at the gaming-table. He might easily have spoiled it:—much of our lives is spent in marring our own influence and turning others’ belief in us into a widely concluding unbelief which they call knowledge of the world, while it is really disappointment in you or me. Deronda had not spoiled his mission.
The narrator reflects on Gwendolen's dependence on Deronda's judgment as her moral compass, suggesting that another person can function as the conscience that prevents self-deception and permits genuine moral growth.
Gwendolen HarlethDaniel DerondaMrs. Davilowconsciencejudgmentmoralitydependencevirtuetragicpolemical
Host (host)
A document. That's a good place to pause — and also exactly the word that leads us to our final question. Because what Gwendolen is left with at the end of the novel is precisely an education that is unfinished — virtue still in the making, as Eliot puts it, with no guarantee of completion. That's where we're going next: what does it mean to call this an education — and whether Eliot thinks it ever ends.

Closing: The Unfinished Education (closing)

Planned questions

James Blackstone
James, let's start with the ending's most famous image — Deronda declaring 'I shall call myself a Jew' and then immediately sailing off to the East. You've described this as Eliot abandoning her own realism. But is that actually a flaw, or is it just... a man with money and a mission, and Eliot is totally clear-eyed about how that works?

But when you read it, can you tell us whether you hear Eliot believing it, or performing it? Because Caroline thinks it sounds 'almost operatic, almost parodic.' Who's right?
Force a direct confrontation between Hartley's reading of the passage as liturgical and Woodcourt's reading of it as sublime-but-slightly-ridiculous; get the passage read aloud for atmosphere
Follow up: Caroline Woodcourt, James Blackstone
Caroline Woodcourt
Caroline, you've called the ending 'aesthetic anesthesia' — beautiful language that stops us noticing what's actually happened to Gwendolen. So I want to put that word 'precious' to you: 'Her remorse was the precious sign of a recoverable nature.' You said that word stops you. Why? What's Eliot doing with it, and does it vindicate the ending or indict it?
Draw out Woodcourt's close-reading instincts on the 'precious' passage; create space for Blackstone to follow up on who exactly gets to decide Gwendolen's remorse is redemptive, and for Hartley to push back with her structural reading of that same sentence

Eleanor Hartley
Eleanor, you've argued that the unfinished ending is the whole point — that Eliot is deliberately blowing up the bildungsroman's promise of a settled self. But James is going to say that's a very generous interpretation of what might just be evasion. So: is there a difference between a novel that refuses closure on principle, and a novel that just... doesn't know how to finish?
Provoke genuine disagreement between Hartley's formal defense of anti-climax and Blackstone's critique of the ending as institutional dodge; also gives Woodcourt an opening to argue that Gwendolen's fate exposes the difference

Caroline Woodcourt
Last question, and I want all three of you on this one: Gwendolen ends the novel 'more solitary and helpless.' Is that George Eliot being devastatingly honest — or is it George Eliot failing her own character? Because there's a version of this where the real unfinished education isn't Deronda's, and isn't even Gwendolen's — it's Eliot's. She couldn't imagine what came next for a woman like that. Could anyone in 1876?
End the segment with a genuinely open question about the limits of the novel's imagination versus its honesty; designed to bring all three experts into direct conversation and allow each to land their strongest final take
Follow up: Eleanor Hartley, James Blackstone

Steering notes

This segment has real combustion built in — Blackstone wants institutional specificity, Hartley wants phenomenological depth, Woodcourt wants to talk about Gwendolen while the other two keep talking about Deronda. The host's job is to keep pulling the conversation back to Gwendolen whenever it drifts into abstract debate about Zionism or vocation. Get passages read aloud early — especially the 'Invisible Power' section and the 'precious sign' sentence — because the prose itself will do a lot of the dramatic work. Let Blackstone and Hartley fight, then bring Woodcourt in as the person who says: yes, but look at who gets left behind while you two are arguing about the man.

Cross-engagement targets

  • Blackstone argues Deronda's declaration is stirringly abstract — Hartley directly disagrees, reading the conditional syntax of 'if there is anything to be done' as deepening rather than weakening the commitment. Get them to read the same sentence at each other.
  • Woodcourt hears the 'Invisible Power' passage as 'almost parodic'; Hartley hears it as 'almost liturgical.' These are not reconcilable readings — push for direct response rather than parallel monologue.
  • All three quote the 'precious sign of a recoverable nature' passage but for opposite purposes: Hartley sees spiritual architecture, Blackstone sees Deronda usurping Gwendolen's self-determination, Woodcourt fixates on the word 'precious' as oddly jewel-like. This is the segment's richest cross-engagement moment — let it run.
  • Blackstone's claim that the ending is 'deeply conservative disguised as spiritual' is a direct provocation to Hartley's argument that the refusal of closure is structurally radical. These are the two positions most worth putting in direct collision.
Host (host)
We've been talking about the novel's double structure — two souls, one book — and that tension doesn't resolve so much as it detonates in the final chapters. So let's talk about endings — what the novel delivers, what it withholds, and who pays the price. James, I want to start with you on the ending's most famous image: Deronda declaring 'I shall call myself a Jew' and sailing east. You've called this Eliot abandoning her own realism. But is that a flaw — or is it simply a man with money and a mission, and Eliot completely clear-eyed about how that works?
Chapter 60, c60:p25
Chapter 60
“I shall call myself a Jew,” said Deronda, deliberately, becoming slightly paler under the piercing eyes of his questioner. “But I will not say that I shall profess to believe exactly as my fathers have believed. Our fathers themselves changed the horizon of their belief and learned of other races. But I think I can maintain my grandfather’s notion of separateness with communication. I hold that my first duty is to my own people, and if there is anything to be done toward restoring or perfecting their common life, I shall make that my vocation.”
Deronda solemnly declares himself a Jew while allowing for doctrinal flexibility, commits to his grandfather's principle of separateness with communication, and articulates his vocation as dedicating himself to the restoration and perfection of Jewish communal life.

James Blackstone (social_historian)
The passage is extraordinary, and I'll grant that much immediately. Deronda stands there, slightly paler under the piercing eyes of his questioner — Eliot gives us that physiological detail, that flash of vulnerability — and then he speaks. 'I hold that my first duty is to my own people, and if there is anything to be done toward restoring or perfecting their common life, I shall make that my vocation.' And what strikes me, every time, is that conditional: 'if there is anything to be done.' For a man declaring a life's vocation, that's an astonishing degree of institutional vagueness. Restoring and perfecting communal life — but which institutions? Which laws? Which levers of power? Eliot is clear-eyed about his money and his privilege, yes. But I'd argue she's less clear-eyed about what he proposes to do with them.

Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
James, I want to push back on that — directly. Because I think you're reading that conditional as weakness, and I read it as precision. 'If there is anything to be done' — that's not evasion. That is a man who has just learned he is Jewish in the last months refusing to arrive with a blueprint. Eliot is doing something structurally quite brave here — she's writing a bildungsroman that refuses the genre's central promise. The hero reaches the end of his formation and he doesn't arrive. He sets out. That syntactic hedge — 'if there is anything' — is the sound of a genuinely open future. For Eliot, that's radical.

James Blackstone (social_historian)
It's a generous reading. And I can admire the formal argument even while remaining suspicious. Because a refusal of closure can be structurally radical and politically conservative at the same time. Those aren't mutually exclusive. Deronda sails east on inherited wealth and inherited connections. The vocation is stirring and the destination is vague — and that combination, historically, tends to work out very well for the man with the money.

Chapter 69, c69:p48
Chapter 69
There was a long silence between them. The world seemed getting larger round poor Gwendolen, and she more solitary and helpless in the midst. The thought that he might come back after going to the East, sank before the bewildering vision of these wild-stretching purposes in which she felt herself reduced to a mere speck. There comes a terrible moment to many souls when the great movements of the world, the larger destinies of mankind, which have lain aloof in newspapers and other neglected reading, enter like an earthquake into their own lives—where the slow urgency of growing generations turns into the tread of an invading army or the dire clash of civil war, and gray fathers know nothing to seek for but the corpses of their blooming sons, and girls forget all vanity to make lint and bandages which may serve for the shattered limbs of their betrothed husbands. Then it is as if the Invisible Power that had been the object of lip-worship and lip-resignation became visible, according to the imagery of the Hebrew poet, making the flames his chariot, and riding on the wings of the wind, till the mountains smoke and the plains shudder under the rolling fiery visitations. Often the good cause seems to lie prostrate under the thunder of unrelenting force, the martyrs live reviled, they die, and no angel is seen holding forth the crown and the palm branch. Then it is that the submission of the soul to the Highest is tested, and even in the eyes of frivolity life looks out from the scene of human struggle with the awful face of duty, and a religion shows itself which is something else than a private consolation.
Eliot reflects on how great movements of history and duty suddenly invade private lives, transforming frivolity into moral urgency and private comfort into submission to the Highest.
Gwendolen Harlethdutylovehistorywarmoralityreligiontragicpolemical
Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
I'll read it. And I'm going to read it slowly, because rushing this prose is a crime. Eliot writes of the moment when history enters private life — she calls it 'a terrible moment' — and then she builds to this: 'Then it is as if the Invisible Power that had been the object of lip-worship and lip-resignation became visible, according to the imagery of the Hebrew poet, making the flames his chariot, and riding on the wings of the wind, till the mountains smoke and the plains shudder under the rolling fiery visitations.' That is liturgical. That is Eliot writing at the full pitch of her prose — and she knows exactly what she's doing. The phrase 'lip-worship and lip-resignation' — that's a devastating critique of a whole class of people for whom religion is decorative. And then she makes the invisible visible — quite literally, in the syntax. That's not parody. That's controlled ecstasy.

Caroline Woodcourt (close_reader)
I don't entirely disagree — and I want to say that carefully, because 'almost parodic' was perhaps too strong. But notice who this is happening to. It's happening to Gwendolen. Eliot writes that 'the world seemed getting larger round poor Gwendolen, and she more solitary and helpless in the midst.' And then we get this magnificent cosmic vision — flames, chariots, mountains smoking — and it's the sublime backdrop to her abandonment. Eleanor is right that it's controlled ecstasy. But controlled ecstasy is also a way of not looking too directly at a woman being left behind. The prose performs elevation. Gwendolen experiences devastation. Those two things are happening simultaneously.
