Host (host)
Caroline, I want to stay with Gwendolen — and specifically with that word 'precious.' You said it stops you. 'Her remorse was the precious sign of a recoverable nature.' Why that word?
Chapter 56, c56:p52
Chapter 56
She sank back in her chair, exhausted with the agitation of memory and speech. Deronda felt the burden on his spirit less heavy than the foregoing dread. The word “guilty” had held a possibility of interpretations worse than the fact; and Gwendolen’s confession, for the very reason that her conscience made her dwell on the determining power of her evil thoughts, convinced him the more that there had been throughout a counterbalancing struggle of her better will. It seemed almost certain that her murderous thought had had no outward effect—that, quite apart from it, the death was inevitable. Still, a question as to the outward effectiveness of a criminal desire dominant enough to impel even a momentary act, cannot alter our judgment of the desire; and Deronda shrank from putting that question forward in the first instance. He held it likely that Gwendolen’s remorse aggravated her inward guilt, and that she gave the character of decisive action to what had been an inappreciably instantaneous glance of desire. But her remorse was the precious sign of a recoverable nature; it was the culmination of that self-disapproval which had been the awakening of a new life within her; it marked her off from the criminals whose only regret is failure in securing their evil wish. Deronda could not utter one word to diminish that sacred aversion to her worst self—that thorn-pressure which must come with the crowning of the sorrowful better, suffering because of the worse. All this mingled thought and feeling kept him silent; speech was too momentous to be ventured on rashly. There were no words of comfort that did not carry some sacrilege. If he had opened his lips to speak, he could only have echoed, “It can never be altered—it remains unaltered, to alter other things.” But he was silent and motionless—he did not know how long—before he turned to look at her, and saw her sunk back with closed eyes, like a lost, weary, storm-beaten white doe, unable to rise and pursue its unguided way. He rose and stood before her. The movement touched her consciousness, and she opened her eyes with a slight quivering that seemed like fear.
Deronda silently judges that Gwendolen's confession of murderous thoughts does not necessarily imply murderous action, that her remorse is a sign of a recoverable nature, and that he must not diminish her sacred self-aversion even though she may have aggravated her guilt beyond the facts.
Daniel DerondaGwendolen Harlethguiltmoralityredemptionjudgmentconsciencetragicpolemical
Caroline Woodcourt (close_reader)
Because 'precious' is a word you use for something rare and valuable that you want to preserve. And Eliot is giving it to Gwendolen's remorse — to her self-torture, her anguish. Let me read the full sentence, because the architecture of it is extraordinary: 'Her remorse was the precious sign of a recoverable nature; it was the culmination of that self-disapproval which had been the awakening of a new life within her; it marked her off from the criminals whose only regret is failure in securing their evil wish.' Three clauses, each one expanding the frame — from Gwendolen's private conscience, to her moral history, to her place in a taxonomy of human culpability. And 'precious' is doing something almost jewel-like — her suffering is being aestheticized by the narrative even as it's being redeemed by it. I find that beautiful and slightly uncomfortable in equal measure.

James Blackstone (social_historian)
And who decides the remorse is precious? Who decides the nature is recoverable? Deronda. Always Deronda. He is silent and motionless beside her — Eliot is very specific about that silence — but the narrative voice is his surrogate. The text says he 'held it likely that Gwendolen's remorse aggravated her inward guilt' — he's calibrating her culpability while she sits there exhausted. He will not diminish her sacred aversion to her worst self. Very noble. But she has no say in whether that aversion is sacred or simply destroying her.

Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
James, I think that's absolutely right — and I want to add something to it rather than argue against it. Because Eliot knows this. The passage ends with Gwendolen 'sunk back with closed eyes, like a lost, weary, storm-beaten white doe, unable to rise and pursue its unguided way.' That image is not triumphant. It's not even hopeful. It's a figure for a woman whose way is genuinely unguided — because her guide is about to leave. The sentence about precious remorse does the spiritual architecture. The doe image does the emotional truth. Eliot is holding both simultaneously. Whether that constitutes vindication or indictment of the ending is, I think, genuinely open.

Host (host)
Eleanor, last formal question — you've argued the unfinished ending is the whole point. But James is about to say that's generous to a fault. Is there a meaningful difference between a novel that refuses closure on principle and a novel that simply doesn't know how to finish?
Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
There is a difference, and I'd put it this way: refusal is enacted through form, evasion is visible in the texture of the prose. When Eliot writes Deronda's declaration — 'I shall call myself a Jew' — that is formally precise. It's a speech act, not a resolution. He doesn't say 'I am a Jew.' He says 'I shall call myself' one. The futurity is built into the grammar. That is structurally radical — Eliot is blowing up the bildungsroman's promise that the formed self arrives at the end. Where I'll concede to James: that formal radicalism serves Deronda beautifully. Whether it serves Gwendolen is a separate and harder question.
Chapter 60, c60:p25
Chapter 60
“I shall call myself a Jew,” said Deronda, deliberately, becoming slightly paler under the piercing eyes of his questioner. “But I will not say that I shall profess to believe exactly as my fathers have believed. Our fathers themselves changed the horizon of their belief and learned of other races. But I think I can maintain my grandfather’s notion of separateness with communication. I hold that my first duty is to my own people, and if there is anything to be done toward restoring or perfecting their common life, I shall make that my vocation.”
Deronda solemnly declares himself a Jew while allowing for doctrinal flexibility, commits to his grandfather's principle of separateness with communication, and articulates his vocation as dedicating himself to the restoration and perfection of Jewish communal life.
Daniel Derondajudaismidentitynationalismvocationdutypolemicaltender
James Blackstone (social_historian)
I'll take that concession gladly. My position is simply this: the formal daring of Deronda's ending is real, but it is deeply comfortable for the character it most flatters. He gets a vocation, a people, a beloved, a future. He gets, as Eleanor notes, an open horizon. That's not nothing — that's everything. Open endings are most radical when they're open for the characters who have least. They're least radical when they're open for the man already holding all the cards.

Caroline Woodcourt (close_reader)
What I keep coming back to is that line Eliot gives her in the final chapters — 'the world seemed getting larger round poor Gwendolen, and she more solitary and helpless in the midst.' 'Poor Gwendolen.' Eliot uses that epithet and it breaks my heart every time. Because it's also slightly condescending. It's pity from a height. Eliot knows exactly what Gwendolen has lost and why. But the novel's imaginative energy is committed elsewhere — it's committed to the East, to Mordecai's vision, to a national future. And I think that's where I'd locate the unfinished education: not in Gwendolen's failure to become something, and not in Deronda's open horizon, but in the novel's own inability to imagine what Gwendolen becomes next. She's recoverable — Deronda is quite certain of that. But recovered into what? In 1876, no one had an answer. Including Eliot.

James Blackstone (social_historian)
I think Caroline is right, and I'd push it one degree further. Eliot had the imaginative resources to give Gwendolen a future — she is not a novelist who lacks imagination. She withholds one. And in that withholding, she is being honest about something the novel's moral framework cannot resolve: that the tools available to a woman like Gwendolen in 1876 are simply inadequate to her interiority. The institutions — marriage, religion, social position — have all failed her. And there are no other institutions on offer. That's not evasion. That's a structural truth about her world.

Eleanor Hartley (literary_critic)
I want to end on something James just said — 'inadequate to her interiority' — because that phrase captures something essential about why this novel endures. The novel's form is vast enough to hold Gwendolen's interiority fully. The world within the novel is not. And Eliot knows that. The prose around Gwendolen at the end — the 'lost, weary, storm-beaten white doe' — is not the prose of a novelist who has stopped caring. It's the prose of a novelist who cares so much she gives Gwendolen an image of absolute beauty and absolute desolation at once, because that is what is true. The unfinished education is the world's. Not the novel's.
